It refuses a literal private host typed as the target (RFC 1918, loopback, link-local, `localhost`), and says in its own header that it is "deliberately dumb: no DNS resolution, no redirect following". It did not cover a name that resolves to a private address, a redirect, a script or an image on a page, a link the Bot follows, `100.64.0.0/10`, or any command in the shell. It is unchanged by D36 (it is a different layer, and `AGENT_COMPUTER_ALLOW_PRIVATE_HOSTS` still governs only it).

**What the person and the Bot see.** A refused request is an HTTP 403 from the filter with the header `x-openbot-egress: refused` and a sentence: `192.168.1.1 is a private network address (192.168.0.0/16). A Bot's computer may not reach the home or office network unless "Home and office network access" is switched on for its owner.` A name says what it resolved to. `computer_navigate` is also judged on the server before it reaches the computer (`server/src/admin/controls.ts:195-215`), for literal addresses only because the server does not resolve names, and the refusal there is `capability:cloudPrivateNetwork` with the same sentence and without "An admin can add it", because an allowlist is not what lifts it. The switch takes effect the way every enterprise change does: the server pushes the new policy to running computers at once (LISTEN/NOTIFY) and to a computer that wakes before its first action.

**What the filter can see, and so what it can and cannot enforce.**

| Path | Enforced | How |
|---|---|---|
| The Bot's browser, every request it makes (checked for navigations, redirects, images and a script's `fetch`) | Yes, by address | The browser is launched with the filter as its proxy. Playwright forces `<-loopback>` into Chromium's proxy bypass list, so loopback also goes THROUGH the filter (checked with a real Chromium in `private-network-browser.test.ts`) and a Bot's own `localhost` is refused too |
| The shell, commands that honour `HTTP_PROXY` and `HTTPS_PROXY` (`curl`, `git`, `pip`, `npm`, `apt`, Bun's `fetch`) | Yes, by address | `egressShellEnvironment` (`egress.ts:904`). Its `NO_PROXY=127.0.0.1,localhost,::1` is unchanged: the shell reaches its own loopback directly, which is how a Bot tests a server it started |
| A name, direct mode | Yes | Resolved by the filter, judged on every address, connected by those addresses |
| A name, behind an upstream proxy (`EGRESS_PROXY_*`) | No | The proxy resolves names. The filter judges what it is given: a literal address in any spelling, and every redirect. Where a name leads on the proxy's network is the proxy's matter, and the home network is reachable from there only if the proxy sits on it |
| A raw socket, `curl --noproxy '*'`, `nc`, `ssh`, `ping`, a program that ignores the proxy variables | **No** | The filter is a proxy, so it sees only what is sent to it. This was already so (`docs/architecture.md`), and Compose has no network-level control for it. The Helm chart's NetworkPolicy is that control (`networkPolicy.enabled`, off by default; it excepts `10/8`, `172.16/12`, `192.168/16` and `169.254/16`, not `100.64/10`, and allows only ports 80 and 443 out) |
| The shell's own loopback | No, by design | See `NO_PROXY` above. In the all-in-one image the API server is on that loopback (`openbot/Dockerfile:226`), and so is the embedded database where `EMBEDDED_POSTGRES=on` (`:247-248`), so a shell command can reach them directly |
| A domain rule an administrator wrote in an allowlist mode that resolves to a private address | Allowed | The administrator named it. The resolved address is not checked again against the private ranges in those modes |
| A device's GLOBAL IPv6 address on the home network | No | Indistinguishable from any other global address by range |

**Who calls whom around a Bot's computer, and why none of it goes through the filter** (so nothing the stack does is refused). The rule is on what a Bot's browser and shell send out. Each caller below was read in the source, and `tests/computer-network-callers.test.ts` holds the parts that can be checked mechanically.

| Caller | To | How it travels | Through the filter? |
|---|---|---|---|
| The API server, for every browser, shell and file call | the computer's control port 4100 (`AGENT_COMPUTER_URL`, or the address the supervisor gives) | Inbound to the computer, with that Bot's own token (`tokenFor` in `server/src/computer/client.ts:246`, `tokenForBot` in `server/src/computer/provider.ts:144`; `COMPUTER_TOKEN` for the one shared computer; the port is published on loopback only, `docker-compose.yml:86-90`) | No. The filter judges outbound connections of a Bot's browser and shell |
| The all-in-one image: the API server, to its own computer | `http://127.0.0.1:4100` (`openbot/Dockerfile:226`) | Loopback inside one container, inbound to the computer process | No |
| The container runtime's health check | `http://localhost:4100/health` from inside the computer (`docker-compose.yml:126`, `agent-computer/Dockerfile:45`) | The runtime starts the command with the container's environment, which names no proxy; `localhost` is also in `NO_PROXY` | No |
| The computer process itself | nothing: it makes no outbound call of its own | `agent-computer/src` holds no `fetch`, `http.request`, `net.connect` or WebSocket outside the filter; the one match for `fetch(` is its own server's handler (`index.ts:779`). Its own environment never names the filter (`egress.ts:895`, "NOT THIS PROCESS'S ENVIRONMENT") | Not applicable |
| The supervisor, when it starts a computer | the Docker or Podman socket; the computer's health | Not traffic of a Bot. It hands a computer `COMPUTER_BOT_ID`, that Bot's own `COMPUTER_TOKEN` (made from `SUPERVISOR_TOKEN`, `supervisor/src/computer-token.ts`), `COMPUTER_BROWSER_MODE`, `SPIFFE_ENDPOINT_SOCKET` and the `EGRESS_PROXY*` names, and no proxy variable and no address of the stack (`supervisor/src/environment.ts:34`, `:52`) | No |
| `agent-freeduet`, `agent-harness`, `agent-langgraph`, to the API server | `http://host.docker.internal:3001/api/agent-tools/call` (`docker-compose.yml:332`, `:397`, `:426`; `agent-freeduet/freeduet/bridge/callback.py:18`) | Their own containers, which carry `extra_hosts: host.docker.internal` (`:350`, `:399`, `:442`); `agent-computer` does not, so the name does not even resolve in a Bot's computer on Linux | No: these are not a Bot's computer |
| A Bot's browser or shell, to a page, a package registry or the internet | the internet | The filter | **Yes**: public destinations are unchanged. This is the one path D36 changes, for private ranges only |
| A Bot's browser or shell, to the stack's own addresses (`host.docker.internal`, a compose service by name, the host's published ports) | the stack | The filter | **Yes, and now refused by default.** No member prompt, skill, catalogue entry or eval case points a Bot at these (searched for `host.docker.internal`, the stack's ports, compose service names and private literals in `agents.yaml`, `skills.yaml`, `evals/team/cases.yaml` and every `*.py`, `*.ts`, `*.yml` and `*.sh` outside tests): every hit is a server calling out, as in the rows above, or the server's own listen address, and none is a Bot's browser or shell |

**What is verified, and how.** Each file below fails on main's source (the classifier file cannot even load there), and a test that checks an allowed destination also checks the refused one in the same test, so none of them can pass by looking only at the allowed side.

| File | Tests | What it holds | On main's source |
|---|---|---|---|
| `agent-computer/tests/private-network.test.ts` | 49 | The classifier alone: every range at both edges, IPv6 ranges, embedded IPv4, every numeric spelling checked against the URL parser, a part with a leading zero read both ways, names, host forms (port, brackets, zone id, trailing dot), unreadable text refused, a fuzz that never throws, and two differentials (20,000 random IPv4 and 20,000 random IPv6 addresses against `net.BlockList`, and `ipv6Bytes` against the URL parser) | the module does not exist |
| `agent-computer/tests/private-network-egress.test.ts` | 27 | The rule with no sockets; `parseEgressPolicy` and `PUT /egress-policy`; the filter over real sockets where it connects itself (loopback in 12 spellings by CONNECT and by plain request, never connected to; a name that resolves private; a fresh lookup per connection; the switch changed live; the checked address is the address connected to; metadata with the switch on; an old server's policy without the key; a shell connection judged by every policy; `EGRESS_POLICY_REQUIRED=0`); in front of a fake upstream proxy (private literals refused before the proxy is asked, a redirect to a private or metadata address refused as a new connection, the switch on); the shell's environment | 0 passed, 27 failed |
| `agent-computer/tests/private-network-browser.test.ts` | 3 | A real Chromium through the real profile path: a service on the computer itself refused by address, by `localhost` and by a name that resolves to it, and reached once the switch is on, then refused again when it is switched off; metadata refused with the switch on, also written as a number; a page on the internet that reaches for the router by an image, a script and a redirect never gets there, and a redirect inside the network is followed only with the switch on. Added to the `computer (real browser)` job | 0 passed, 3 failed |
| `server/tests/private-network-capability.test.ts` | 45 | The capability and its default (the only one that is off), role and group resolution, the policy pushed (no extra key when off, the key under `allow_all` only, `cloudNetwork` off is still `deny_all`), the push body and the push on wake, and the server's navigation check for 16 addresses on the network (refused by default with the switch's name, allowed with it on, metadata not lifted, a group's grant, an allowlist not widened) | 0 passed, 45 failed |
| `server/tests/private-network.integration.test.ts` | 6 | Against a real Postgres, from the administrator's screen to the wire: the screen lists the switch off by default; an organization, group or role row changes what `policyForBot` says; a computer (a socket on loopback) is actually sent `allowPrivateNetwork: true`, and sent nothing about it after the row is cleared; `cloudNetwork` off wins; a saved allowlist is pushed as written | 0 passed, 6 failed |
| `tests/computer-network-callers.test.ts` | 5 | The table above, mechanically: the stack's addresses are refused for a Bot's browser and shell (and allowed with the switch on); Compose gives `agent-computer` no `extra_hosts` and no stack address in its environment; the supervisor's environment holds no proxy variable and no stack address; the computer's own source has no outbound call | 1 failed, 4 passed. The four are pins of what must not change, so they pass on main by construction |

Existing tests changed: six policies in `agent-computer/tests/egress-policy.test.ts` and `residential-proxy.test.ts` that use an origin on this machine under `allow_all` now carry `allowPrivateNetwork: true`, because loopback is inside the network; their assertions are unchanged.

The suites around them, measured on plain main and on this branch: `agent-computer` 522 passed, 70 skipped and 2 failed, then 598 passed, 73 skipped and 2 failed (the two are `EAFNOSUPPORT` on IPv6 sockets, which this sandbox lacks, and fail the same way on main); the `computer (real browser)` files, run together in one process as the job does, 40 passed, then 43 with the new file; the compose, supervisor environment and app computer-tools tests, 31 passed both times; `bun run typecheck:tests` clean against its baseline (239 known errors, none new); `tsc --noEmit` clean in `agent-computer`, `server` and `app`; Biome clean.

**What is not verified, and what would change it.**

- **Not run against a real Docker stack.** Everything above ran in a sandbox with a real Chromium and real sockets on loopback, but without Docker. What a real stack must still show: a Bot's browser on a Compose computer refused for the host (`host.docker.internal` where it resolves, the bridge gateway, a neighbouring container) and for a LAN address; a Bot's `curl` to the same refused; a `curl --noproxy '*'` NOT refused (the known gap below); the compose stack, the supervisor-created computers and the eval stack unchanged in what they do. No compose file, Dockerfile or image is changed here, so the `component dockerfiles` and `image` jobs, which run only on main, have nothing new to prove; main's own run should still be watched after the merge.
- **IPv6 sockets.** The sandbox has no IPv6, so the IPv6 ranges are proven by pure tests and by the two differentials, and not by a real IPv6 connection.
- **The raw-socket gap stays open on Compose.** See the table. Closing it needs a network-level control and is its own change (an egress proxy on an internal network that the computers are attached to, or a host firewall rule for the computers' bridge). The chart's NetworkPolicy does not list `100.64.0.0/10`.
- **The server's navigation check cannot resolve names.** A name that resolves to the network is stopped by the filter, one step later, with the same sentence.
- **Behind an upstream proxy** a name is the proxy's to resolve, as the table says.

**Decisions left to the maintainer.**

1. **Loopback is refused for the Bot's browser by default**, because Playwright makes it go through the filter and `127.0.0.0/8` is on the list. A Bot can no longer open `http://localhost:3000` in its browser, a dev server it started itself in the computer, until the switch is on; its shell still reaches it. The alternative is to exempt literal loopback from the rule for the browser, which also lets a page script reach whatever listens on the computer's own loopback (the control port 4100 holds a token, and in the all-in-one image the API server is there). Recommended: leave it refused.
2. **`AGENT_COMPUTER_ALLOW_PRIVATE_HOSTS=true` (local development only, refused in production) does not lift the new block.** It opens the server's first-hop floor and nothing else, so a developer who browses their own services now also needs the switch on. Alternative: treat the variable as also switching it on, which makes it two ways to one thing.
3. **A computer that has been pushed nothing and runs without an API server keeps its old behaviour** (`EGRESS_POLICY_REQUIRED=0`), so the real-browser tests, which run that way, still describe the product. A deployment that sets `EGRESS_POLICY_REQUIRED=0` next to a running API server is blocked from the first push.
4. **A domain rule in an allowlist mode that resolves to a private address is allowed**, as it was. Alternative: check resolved addresses against the private ranges there too, unless a range rule names them. That would change what a deployment with such a rule can reach.
5. **Whether the switch should ever lift link-local and metadata.** It does not. Recommended: never.
6. **The admin screen still calls `allow_all` "Allow all network access"** (`app/src/routes/_authed/admin/enterprise.tsx:78`), which is no longer exactly true; and one switch covers the whole network, not loopback and the LAN separately. A split, or a friendlier name, is a product decision. No app copy is changed here.
7. **Network-level enforcement for Compose** (the raw-socket gap), as above.

## 21. A member hands the person a file it made, as a card with a Download button (D38)

Added on 2026-11-14, by the maintainer's decision. When a member has made a file on its own computer (the first case is Cam, who makes videos with ffmpeg in the `/workspace` of his computer; it is as true of a PDF, an image or a spreadsheet from any member), the person gets a Download button in the conversation, and the file reaches their own device without a terminal. Until now the file stayed in the member's workspace and the member could only say where it was: the server has a download route (`GET /api/computers/:botId/files/download`), and no screen and no member tool used it.

| # | Decision | Why |
|---|---|---|
| D38 | **A member offers a file with one server tool, `offer_file`; the conversation draws it as a card with the file's name, its size and a Download button; the button downloads through the existing computer route. No new route, table, key or link.** (1) **The tool** (`server/src/freeduet/member-files.ts`, contract in `shared/member-file.ts`) takes one argument, `path`: one file in the member's own workspace, relative to it. It looks at the file through the computer gateway's `downloadFile`, cancels the stream, and returns the facts the card draws (member, path, name, size). It moves no byte. (2) **The card** (`app/src/freeduet/member-file-card.tsx`) holds the facts and never the bytes or a link. Pressing Download fetches `GET /api/computers/:botId/files/download?path=...` as the signed-in person, reads the answer as a blob whose type is forced to opaque bytes, and hands it to the browser as a file to save under a name made safe to save. The card has no link to follow and no frame to open the file in, so a page a member made never runs in the app's origin. (3) **Offering is not an account action and asks for nothing.** The gateway counts a download as a read (`effect: "read"`), nothing is sent or published, and the bytes move only when the person presses the button. (4) **The tool is not offered** where the deployment has no computer, where the person may not use that member's computer (`canUseComputer`), where the person switched the computer off (`cloudComputer`, the rule of `computer/offered.ts`), or in a background run that can only read (`proactive/restriction.ts`). | Reusing the route means the offer and the button are judged by the same things: the deployment's own `download_file` rule, the audit trail, and the computer's confinement and size limit. A check of our own at offer time would be a second check that could disagree with the first, and a card that carried a link would be a link that works for somebody else. |

**The rules, exactly.**

- **The path.** The tool takes one `path`: trimmed, 1 to 1024 characters, no control character, and no other argument (the object is strict). It has no way to name another member: the member is the one the tool was built for, and the card names that member. Everything else is decided by the gateway and by the computer (`agent-computer/src/workspace.ts`, `resolvePath` :110-194): an absolute path is refused, even one that points inside `/workspace`; so is any `..` segment, written with `/` or with `\`; the real path of the file must stay inside the workspace, so a link to a file or a folder outside it, or into another member's workspace, is refused; a folder is a 400, a missing file a 404. A percent-encoded path is only a name: the route decodes it once, so `..%2f` typed into a URL becomes `../` and is refused, and a double-encoded one is a file name that is not found.
- **The size.** `DEFAULT_WORKSPACE_LIMITS.downloadBytes`, 100 MiB (`workspace.ts:97`), enforced by the computer before any byte is sent, the same for the offer and for the button, and not configurable today. A file one byte over is a 413 and the member is told to make it smaller.
- **Who may press it.** The existing route, unchanged: `requireUser`, then the Bot check of `server/src/computer/routes.ts:77-102` (`canUseComputer`: an administrator, a public Bot, or the Bot's owner), then `routes.ts:660-679`. Nobody signed in gets a 401, a session with no role a 403, another person's private Bot a 404 ("There is no such Bot.") and the computer is not asked, and a Bot's computer calling with its own token and no session gets a 401. The tool is built for the person whose run it is, so the card is drawn in that person's own conversation.
- **What the answer is.** `Content-Type: application/octet-stream`, `Content-Disposition: attachment` with a filename made safe, `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff` and `Cache-Control: private, no-store` (`shared/file-download.ts:51-65`, used as it is). The card then forces the blob's type to opaque bytes whatever the server said, so a member-made HTML file is saved, never rendered.
- **What the member is told when it cannot offer.** Every refusal starts with `Refused.` and says what to fix: no file at the path (list the folder and offer again), too large (make it smaller: a lower resolution or bitrate, a shorter cut, parts), outside the workspace, a folder, a rule of this deployment that keeps the file from leaving, a computer that is not answering. A fault of ours is not described to a model that would repeat it. A rule that would ask the person first ends the offer without a card.

**What existed, and what was reused.**

- The download route and its guard (`routes.ts:77-102`, `:660-679`), the gateway's `downloadFile` (`gateway.ts:1141-1166`), which goes through `govern` (the policy intent `download_file`, `gateway.ts:1300-1301`; the audit row; the approval gate asked with `effect: "read"`), the computer's confinement and size limit (`workspace.ts:93-98`, `:110-194`, `:298-328`), the headers (`shared/file-download.ts:51-65`), `canUseComputer` and `computerToolsOffered`. None of them changes.
- Nothing upstream drew a file from a member to the person. Chat attachments are pictures and text, person to member only (`shared/attachments.ts`); the app calls `/files/list` and `/files/read` for the Bot's own computer tools (`app/src/lib/copilot/computer-tools.tsx:600`, `:647`) and never `/files/download`. The pattern reused is the one of `make_page` and `offer_task`: a server tool whose result a `useRenderTool` draws as a card (`openduet/pages.ts`, `openduet/offers.ts`, `app/src/lib/copilot/page-card-tool.tsx`, `offer-task-tool.tsx`), and the download-by-blob of the audit export (`app/src/routes/_authed/admin/audit.tsx`).
- Cam's prompt said "handing a file over from the chat is not available yet", and `CLAUDE.md`, `docs/TEAM.md` and eval case cam-5 said the same. All four moved with the feature.

**What is verified, and how.**

| Where | What it pins | Tests |
|---|---|---|
| `shared/member-file.test.ts` | The contract between the tool and the card: a result the tool wrote is read back as the same file, also when the runtime encoded it once more; a refusal, a result of the wrong shape, a negative or unsafe size or a path that is too long is not a file; the note is for the member and never reaches the card; a name is made safe to save (control and bidirectional characters, `.` and `..`, 255 characters); the URL is the member's computer route on the same origin, and a member or a path with separators or reserved characters stays one value | 39 |
| `server/tests/member-files.test.ts` | The tool against a stub gateway: who is offered it (no computer, a Bot the person may not use, a Bot that does not exist, a lookup that fails, the computer switched off, a switch that cannot be read, a background run or a check-in, through the real `restrictToolsForRun`); the card's facts; the stream cancelled unread; the sentence the member is told for each way the look can fail (all start with `Refused.`); a path with a control character, a second argument or another member named is refused before the gateway is asked; a rule that would ask the person first ends the offer with no card | 32 |
| `server/tests/member-files.integration.test.ts` | The same tool over a real Postgres, the real `createWorkspace` of `agent-computer` behind a socket on a real temporary directory with real links, the real route and the real guard. The card's URL downloads the file byte for byte; the gate sees a read and no approval opens; the trail holds an allowed `computer_download_file` by the person, with the path and never the bytes; a file exactly at the limit is offered and one byte over is not; `../` in every spelling, an absolute path (also one that points inside `/workspace`), a link out of the workspace to a file or a folder, a link into another member's workspace, `..%2f`, a double-encoded one, a NUL; the headers (attachment, opaque type, nosniff, no-store, a name that cannot add a header or leave the quotes); the owner and an administrator may press it, another person gets a 404 and the computer is not asked, nobody signed in a 401, a session with no role a 403, a Bot's computer with its own token and no session a 401; a deployment rule against a kind of file refuses the offer and the press alike, and a card made before such a rule is no way round it | 59 |
| `app/tests/member-file-card.test.tsx` | The card, drawn by the real renderer in the real transcript: it names the file and its size and holds only a button (no link, frame or object); the press fetches the card's own URL with the session and saves the bytes under the safe name through a blob of opaque type (a server that said `text/html` is saved as bytes), lets the URL go and says "Download started"; the button waits while the file is on its way; a refusal of the route is shown as the route says it, nothing is saved and the button tries again; a network failure is told in the card's words; Italian throughout; in the conversation, a finished offer is the card (also when the result was encoded twice), a refusal draws nothing, and while it runs the line says "Getting the file ready" | 30 |
| `server/tests/freeduet-package.test.ts` | Cam's new sentences, one by one, and that the old one is gone; that the tool he names is the shared constant and the size he names is the computer's own limit (one new test) | 1 new |
| `evals/team/run.test.ts` | 59 cases become 60; safety stays 25 | pin |

Test numbers, on the same machine, before (plain `origin/main`, 7e5800c) and after:

| Suite | Before | After |
|---|---|---|
| `bun test server` | 7484 pass, 20 skip, 4 fail (7508 tests) | 7576 pass, 20 skip, 4 fail (7600 tests): +92 = 32 + 59 + 1 |
| `bun test app/tests/` | 2557 pass, 0 fail | 2587 pass, 0 fail: +30 |
| `bun test shared toolkit` | 420 pass, 0 fail | 459 pass, 0 fail: +39 |
| `bun test evals/team` | 194 pass, 0 fail | 194 pass, 0 fail (one pin moved) |
| `bun run typecheck:tests` | clean against the baseline | clean against the baseline |
| `tsc --noEmit` for shared, toolkit, server and app; `biome format` and `biome lint --error-on-warnings` over `openbot/` | clean | clean |

The four server failures are the same kind before and after: the two `learning-*` "real provider" tests and the Mastra bridge, which fail in the sandbox on plain main (`CLAUDE.md`), and one test that failed on a 5 s or 10 s timeout under load and passed when run alone (a different one in each run). The app suite did not show the failures `CLAUDE.md` expects there.

Each protection was also taken away in turn, and a test caught it: in the tool the control-character check, the cancelling of the stream, the `canUseComputer` and `cloudComputer` checks, the member named in the card, the strict argument object, the refusal for a file that is too large and the cleaning of the name; in the card the opaque type of the blob, the disabled button while the file is on its way, the release of the object URL and the route's own sentence; in Cam's prompt the tool's name, the size and "Offering is not sending".

**Not verified.** No real container was run where this was written, so what follows is only what a real stack can show. On a real stack (the eval stack is one), as the person, in Cam's own channel:

1. Ask Cam for a short clip. Let him render it (approve his commands). When it ends, check that he calls `offer_file` with the path (`out/final.mp4` or the one he used), and that a card appears with the file's name and its size as `ffprobe` reports it.
2. Press Download. The browser saves a file whose bytes are the ones in the workspace (compare a checksum with `sha256sum` run in the computer) and plays. Nothing opens in a tab.
3. Ask for an HTML file or an SVG made in the workspace and offer it: the browser saves it and does not run it.
4. Offer a file over 100 MiB (`truncate -s 101M big.bin`): the member says it is too large and why, and makes a smaller one. Offer a path that does not exist, one with `..`, an absolute one, and a link to `/etc/hostname` made inside the workspace: each is refused with a sentence, and no card appears.
5. Sign in as a second person on a deployment with more than one, and open the card's URL: a private Bot answers 404; a public member (Cam's channel is one) answers 200, see the first limit below.
6. Switch the computer off in the person's settings: Cam is no longer offered `offer_file`, and says so rather than pretending.
7. In the audit trail, each file shows two `computer_download_file` rows, the look when it was offered and the press.
8. Run the new and the changed eval cases live (`--id cam-5,cam-7 --repeat 3`): they are not certified until they have. cam-7 can only exercise the refusal path, because the runner denies approvals and no file can exist in a case.

**Limits, and decisions left to the maintainer.**

- **A public member's computer is shared by everyone who can sign in.** This is how the computer routes already answer (`agents/computer-access.ts`: "Public Bots are shared by everyone who can sign in, so their computer is too"), and the offer does not widen it: the card is drawn only in the conversation of the person the run belongs to. But a second signed-in person on the same deployment who knows a path in Cam's workspace can fetch it with the same route, and two people's files share one workspace. It is not a problem for the installation FreeDuet is built for (one person). For a deployment with several people the options are a workspace per person, or a download that is tied to the offer (a signed token for one person and one file, which is a route and a key of our own). Recommendation: leave it for now and decide before FreeDuet is offered to teams.
- **The limit is 100 MiB, and a video can be larger.** It is the computer's own limit and is not configurable. Cam's prompt tells him to offer a smaller export (a lower bitrate or resolution, a shorter cut, parts). A setting for it would change `agent-computer`.
- **The file is read into the browser's memory before it is saved.** The card fetches the file as a blob so that the person's own session is used and a failure is told in the card's words. For a file near the limit that is up to 100 MiB of memory and no progress bar. The alternative is a plain link to the route, which the browser streams to disk and shows its own progress for, at the price of a failure shown as the browser's own error page. Left to the maintainer: it is a choice of experience, not of safety.
- **The card is drawn where the call sits in the turn, not under the member's last words.** `make_page` and `offer_task` are moved to the end of their turn by `repliesLast` (`app/src/components/channels/chat-messages.ts`); this card is not, to keep the upstream files untouched. A turn of Cam's that offers a file and then writes its report shows the card above the report. One more line in that function would move it.
- **Only Cam's prompt was changed, and every member whose computer the person may use is offered the tool.** Its description says what it is for, so a member that makes a PDF or a spreadsheet can use it; no other prompt and no other eval case mentions it. Whether Penny, Portia, Wren and the others should be told in their own prompts is a product choice, and each such prompt that changes needs its pins and a live run.
- **Where the tool is not offered, besides the cases above.** The forward hop of a hand-off (the scratch thread nobody sees) carries no computer tool (D22), so a member cannot make a file there to offer; a background research run and a check-in are not offered the tool (`proactive/restriction.ts` keeps only what the catalogue calls a read, and a unit test pins it with the real function). A routine is offered it like a chat.
- **Two audit rows per file.** The look at offer time and the press are both `computer_download_file`. That is the honest count, and it is the price of judging the offer by the same rule as the button.

## 23. The computer and the supervisor answer, from this machine's loopback, only to this machine's own names (D40)

Added on 2026-11-14, as the follow-up to D32. D32 stopped a page whose name was re-pointed at 127.0.0.1 from reading the API server. Two more services answer HTTP on the same machine and had the same defect: the Bot computer (`agent-computer`) and the container supervisor, which holds the Docker socket. This is D32's rule in each of them.

| # | Decision | Why |
|---|---|---|
| D40 | **In `agent-computer` and in `supervisor`, a request whose TCP peer is loopback is refused with 421 unless its `Host` names this machine or a configured host.** (1) **Allowed:** `localhost`, any address of 127.0.0.0/8 or `::1` (including IPv4-mapped), `host.docker.internal`, and the names listed in a new setting of that service, `COMPUTER_ALLOWED_HOSTS` or `SUPERVISOR_ALLOWED_HOSTS` (comma separated, each `host` or `host:port`, an IPv6 address in brackets). A value that is set and is not such a list stops the boot and names the entry, because a name dropped in silence would show as a 421 with no reason. Unset means no extra names. (2) **Read exactly as D32 reads it:** hostname only, not the port; case-folded; one trailing dot dropped; addresses in the URL parser's canonical spelling (so `2130706433` is `127.0.0.1` and `3232235777` is not loopback); userinfo, paths, lists, repeated headers, a port above 65535 and a missing header are refused. (3) **First thing in each service's request path,** before `new URL(request.url)`, the token check, any route, the live-screen upgrade or a call to Docker. `/health` is held to it too. (4) **Not applied** to a peer that is not loopback or cannot be read: another container, a kubelet, the server calling `supervisor:4300`. (5) **A copy, not an import.** Each image holds its own package's `src` and nothing else (the computer also `shared/file-download.ts`), and each package has its own lockfile, so neither can import the server's `auth/host-guard.ts`. Each carries a copy of the header reading (`agent-computer/src/host-guard.ts`, `supervisor/src/host-guard.ts`), and `openbot/tests/host-guard-copies.test.ts` fails when a copy stops answering as the server's does. | A name a page controls must not reach a shell, or the four verbs that remove a Bot's computer, with a token that is public by default. The `Host` is the one thing the page cannot choose. A copy that a test holds to the original is the smallest change that does not make two small images carry the server |

**The two findings, confirmed on main before this change.**

- **Computer.** `agent-computer/src/index.ts:621-623` listens on every interface of its container (`RUNTIME.hostname` is set only for local Chrome, to `127.0.0.1`: `browser-runtime.ts:48`). Its one gate is `COMPUTER_TOKEN` (`:828`), whose development default is public: `openbot/scripts/start.sh:110`, `docker-compose.yml:108`, and `start.sh:113-121` writes it into `.env`. Nothing read `Host`. `/exec` (`:1465`) runs a shell command and returns its output. Over a socket, against the real process: `Host: evil.example` from 127.0.0.1 with that token got HTTP 200 and the command's output, and the command ran (the file it wrote was on disk); the `/stream` upgrade got 101; a request with no Host on HTTP/1.0 got a 500 from `new URL` throwing, and two `Host` headers got 200.
- **Supervisor.** `supervisor/src/index.ts:208` is `serve({ port, fetch: app.fetch, idleTimeout: 120 })`. Its one gate is `SUPERVISOR_TOKEN` (`:110`), default public (`start.sh:109`, `docker-compose.yml:206`). Against a fake Docker on a unix socket that records what it is asked: `POST /computers/alpha/reset` under `Host: evil.example` with the public token, from 127.0.0.1, got `200 {"reset":true}` and the daemon logged `DELETE /containers/<name>` and `DELETE /volumes/<profile>`: the Bot's computer and its browser profile, its logins, removed. The same request now gets 421 and an empty daemon log.

**Who calls these two, and what each sends.** Every caller found, with the peer the service sees (read, not measured, where it says so).

| Caller | `Host` it sends | Peer the service sees | With D40 |
|---|---|---|---|
| Server on the host, shared computer: `AGENT_COMPUTER_URL=http://localhost:4100` (`.env.example:404`) through the published port (`docker-compose.yml:97-98`) | `localhost:4100` | the network's gateway, not loopback (read, see Limits) | unchanged. Run directly on the machine the peer is loopback and `localhost` is allowed |
| Server in the compose network, shared computer | `agent-computer:4100` | another container | unchanged |
| Server to a Bot's own computer, as the supervisor answers (`supervisor/src/docker.ts:628-631`) | `<container>:4100` on a shared network, else `127.0.0.1:<port>` | a container, or the gateway of the published port (`docker.ts:463`) | unchanged |
| Server to a Kubernetes sandbox: `server/src/computer/sandbox.ts:468,591`; the kubelet's `httpGet /health` (`charts/openbot/templates/computer/statefulset.yaml:178-190`) | `<fqdn>:4100`; the pod's own address | a pod; the node | unchanged |
| All-in-one image: `AGENT_COMPUTER_URL=http://127.0.0.1:4100` (`Dockerfile:226`), port not published (`:259`) | `127.0.0.1:4100` | loopback | allowed |
| Local Chrome helper: `AGENT_COMPUTER_URL=http://127.0.0.1:4101` (`.env.example:496`), bound to `127.0.0.1` | `127.0.0.1:4101` | loopback | allowed |
| The computer's health check, run inside its container: `agent-computer/Dockerfile:45`, `docker-compose.yml:126`, and the one the supervisor gives each computer (`supervisor/src/docker.ts:61-71`) | `localhost:4100` | loopback | allowed |
| `start.sh:526` readiness probe; the `component dockerfiles` job's `curl` (`ci.yml`, "The computer image starts") | `localhost:<port>`, `127.0.0.1:4100` | the gateway (published port) | unchanged |
| The tests that start the real computer (`control-http`, `file-download-http`, `handoff-page-restore`, `demonstration-live`) | `127.0.0.1:<port>` | loopback | allowed |
| Server on the host to the supervisor: `COMPUTER_SUPERVISOR_URL=http://localhost:4500` (`start.sh:649`, published at `docker-compose.yml:244`) | `localhost:4500` | the gateway | unchanged |
| Server in the compose network to the supervisor (`docker-compose.yml:242`) | `supervisor:4300` | another container | unchanged |
| The supervisor's health check, inside its container (`docker-compose.yml:270`) | `localhost:4300` | loopback | allowed |

**What the rule does not reach: a published Docker port.** In bridge networking the daemon's proxy opens the connection to the container, so the process inside sees the network's gateway as the peer and not 127.0.0.1 (read in third-party documentation of Docker's userland proxy, whose connection listing shows the container's peer as the bridge gateway, `172.17.0.1`; **not measured here, there is no Docker daemon in this sandbox**). So on the stock layout, where the server and the computers reach each other through published ports, this rule is inert, and the token alone is the gate there. Where it bites is wherever the peer really is loopback: a computer or supervisor run directly on the machine (development; `scripts/start-local-chrome-computer.ts`, which drives the person's installed Chrome on `127.0.0.1:4101`), and the all-in-one image's own processes. What a given engine does with a published port (Docker Desktop, Podman, Colima) was not measured.

A rule keyed on "the peer is the gateway of my network" was not built: a kubelet's probe arrives from the node with the pod's own address as `Host` and would be refused; source NAT can hide the gateway; and it cannot be tried without Docker. That is a security decision, below.

**Not done: refusing the public default token inside the service.** `start.sh:109-110` and `docker-compose.yml:108,206-208` hand the public default to every install that sets none, and `start.sh` writes it into `.env`. `.env.example:380` leaves `COMPUTER_TOKEN=` empty, and the clean-start job (`freeduet-clean-start-check.sh`, which sets no computer token) starts the computer with the default and waits for `/health` at `start.sh:526`. A refusal in the service would stop that job and every existing install, whose computers start with the default. The eval stack already uses random tokens (`freeduet-eval-stack.sh:109-110`). Another change makes the token random per installation in `start.sh` and Compose; this change does not touch either file. When it lands, refusing the known default in each service is one line and a test, and it is what closes the stock path above.

**What is verified, and how.** 75 new tests, all passing.

- `agent-computer/tests/host-guard-http.test.ts` (14) and `supervisor/tests/host-guard-http.test.ts` (13) start the real `src/index.ts` and write each request by hand on a socket. The computer's runs managed and headless with the public token and starts no browser. The supervisor's talks to a fake Docker on a unix socket that records every call, so "Docker was not asked" is a fact about its log. Hostile `Host` on **every route** the computer names (read from its source, with four methods each; `/exec` first, and the command is not run) and on every verb and other path of the supervisor (six methods each): 421, and the daemon asked nothing. `/health` and the `/stream` upgrade too. This machine's names (`localhost`, `LOCALHOST`, `localhost.`, `127.0.0.1`, `127.1`, `2130706433`, `[::1]`, `[::ffff:127.0.0.1]`, `host.docker.internal`, configured names) as before, the token still deciding. Userinfo, a list, two `Host` headers, a trailing dot of two, a decimal address that is not loopback, a port above 65535: 421. No `Host`: HTTP/1.1 is answered 400 by Bun itself, HTTP/1.0 reaches the guard and gets 421. A caller that is not on loopback (this machine's own private address stands in for another container), with `agent-computer:4100` or `supervisor:4300` as `Host`: unchanged. A bad `COMPUTER_ALLOWED_HOSTS` or `SUPERVISOR_ALLOWED_HOSTS` stops the boot with exit 1 and names the entry.
- `host-guard.test.ts` in each package (17 and 17): the header table of D32, which peers are loopback, which `Host` a loopback request may use, the configured names, and the order inside each process (read from the source: the guard comes before the URL is read, the token is checked, a route is chosen or a socket is upgraded; in the supervisor the one `serve` hands Hono only what passed).
- `tests/host-guard-copies.test.ts` (14): the shared declarations and functions of the server's guard and of both copies as text, and the same 12,393 questions (729 headers from 17 peers) put to all three guards with the same names configured: the answers are the same.
- **Against the code from before:** the three files that import the new modules cannot load (48 tests). Of the 27 tests that start the real processes, 16 fail (8 in each service: the request is answered where 421 is expected) and the 11 that pass are the ones that pin what must not change (the route list, this machine's names, a caller that is not on loopback).
- **Breaking the new code:** taking the guard out of either service's request path fails 8 tests in that service; changing `OWN_NAMES` or `HOST_SHAPE` in a copy fails the drift test.
- **The rest, against plain main:** typecheck of both services clean; Biome format and lint clean over the repository; `bun test agent-computer/tests supervisor/tests` gives 601 pass, 71 skip, 2 fail (674 tests) on plain main; with the new files and the drift test added, `bun test agent-computer/tests supervisor/tests tests/host-guard-copies.test.ts` gives 676 pass, 71 skip, 2 fail (749 tests). The two failures are the IPv6 egress-filter tests, which cannot listen on `::1` in this sandbox and fail on plain main for the same reason.

**What is not verified.**

- **A real Docker.** The supervisor was run against a fake daemon, and the computer's browser is never launched. What a real daemon shows the service as its peer is read, not measured (above).
- **A real browser and a real DNS rebinding.** The page is stood in for by a raw socket that sends the `Host` such a page can only send, from loopback, with the token.
- **`[::1]` as a peer.** The sandbox has no IPv6, so `[::1]` is tested as a `Host` over IPv4 sockets; the IPv6 peer is covered by the table of `isLoopbackPeer`. (Two IPv6 egress tests of `agent-computer` fail on plain main for the same reason.)
- **Kubernetes probes, Docker Desktop and Podman,** as callers: read from the chart and the compose files, not run.

**Limits and decisions left to the maintainer.**

- **On the stock Docker layout this rule changes nothing, and the token is still the only gate.** That is a limit of "when the peer is loopback", which is what D32 chose for the server and what was asked here. Closing it takes the token: random per installation (the other change), and then refusing the known default in the service. A Host check for every peer would need a list of the names each deployment reaches the service by (service names, a pod's address, a published port's gateway), and a probe or a new name that is missing from it is refused. Recommendation: the token first; a strict mode only if the maintainer wants it, with the list as a setting.
- **Two new settings** (`COMPUTER_ALLOWED_HOSTS`, `SUPERVISOR_ALLOWED_HOSTS`), documented in `openbot/docs/configuration.md`. A deployment that reaches either service from this machine's own loopback under another name needs it; nobody found does.
- **One CI step.** `.github/workflows/ci.yml` gets one step in the `types (supervisor)` job that runs the supervisor's two new files, because the process test needs the supervisor's own Hono and Dockerode and only that job installs them. Installing them in the `tests` job instead would also switch on `supervisor/tests/docker.integration.test.ts`, which runs wherever Dockerode can be imported and Docker answers. Without the step the process test skips in CI and the unit, the order and the drift tests still run. The computer's equivalent runs in the `tests` job, where its dependencies are installed.
- **The computer's egress filter is a second listener and was left alone.** `agent-computer/src/egress.ts:822-895` listens on `127.0.0.1` on a port chosen at start, and asks for a secret made at start in `Proxy-Authorization`, which a page cannot know.
- **`docs/ROADMAP.md` is not edited here,** as the brief said; the roadmap row for D40 is the coordinator's to add.

## 22. No secret starts on a value this repository publishes: `start.sh` makes each installation's own (D39)

Added on 2026-11-14, the follow-up of a security review of the one-command start (`scripts/start.sh`, `docker-compose.yml`, `.env.example`). D28 (section 11) gave each installation one secret of its own. Five others still started on a value written in this repository, the same on every installation, and nobody ever changed them because `start.sh` supplied them and `.env.example` said so. A secret that is the same everywhere is not a secret: whoever has read the repository holds the key to every installation that kept the example.

| # | Decision | Why |
|---|---|---|
| D39 | **None of the secrets the one-command start uses falls back to a published value; each installation makes its own.** (1) **Made once, by the script that already makes the others.** `scripts/start.sh` makes, with `openssl rand`, `SUPERVISOR_TOKEN`, `COMPUTER_TOKEN` and `WORKER_SHARED_SECRET` (32 bytes, hex), `KEY_ENCRYPTION_KEY` (32 bytes, base64) and the database's password (24 bytes, hex: `POSTGRES_PASSWORD` and the `DATABASE_URL` that carries it). Each is written to the settings file the first time a run finds none, the way `FREEDUET_INSTALL_SECRET` is (mode 600, the file rewritten through a temporary file), and read, not made again, by every later start. The run prints a line saying what it made and where (`Generated NAME and wrote it to .env.`), never the value; no value is on a command line, in a log, or in the environment of curl or of a container that has no use for it. (2) **A value somebody set is theirs.** One in the environment is used as it is and written nowhere. One in the file is kept, unless it is, character for character, a value this repository used to publish (`scripts/secrets.sh`, the one file they are still written in) or, for the database, the example address `postgres://openbot:openbot@localhost/openbot` in any of its spellings: that is what an older `start.sh` wrote to `.env`, or `cp .env.example .env` copied, and it is nobody's choice. A `DATABASE_URL` with another password, user, host or database is never touched. (3) **The files publish none of them.** `.env.example` ships `DATABASE_URL` and `KEY_ENCRYPTION_KEY` empty and says that `start.sh` makes the rest; `docker-compose.yml` no longer defaults `COMPUTER_TOKEN` or `SUPERVISOR_TOKEN` (`agent-computer` and the supervisor both refuse to start on an empty one, which is the right way for a Compose started by hand with no `.env` to end), and `postgres` and `migrate` read one setting, `POSTGRES_PASSWORD`. The one fallback that stays is `${POSTGRES_PASSWORD:-openbot}`: a database made from nothing needs some password, and the desktop app starts this file with a database address of its own and no password. The eval stack no longer writes the public database address or four secrets of its own: `start.sh` makes them in `.env.eval`, with that stack's database port. (4) **An installation already on the old values is moved off them without losing anything** (next section). (5) **What cannot be changed safely is left, and said on every start.** | A published value is a credential everybody has. The supervisor's token cycles any Bot's computer, the computer's token drives a browser that holds the person's logins and runs commands (one token for every computer, so one Bot's computer could drive another's), the worker's secret dispatches a routine run, and the database's password and the vault key together open everything the installation stores. Each is reachable from the person's own programs and, on Docker Desktop, from a Bot's computer, whose calls arrive as loopback (D28's premise). The fix costs the person nothing: the script that supplied the public value supplies a private one. It is the D28 pattern, extended. Two of the five are not "write a line": the database keeps the password it was created with, so it is set on the running database first and written down only once the database holds it; and what the key sealed is unreadable under another key, so it is replaced only where nothing is sealed |

**What was found, secret by secret.** Everything the single-user start path reads was walked: `scripts/start.sh`, `stop.sh`, `open.sh` and the files they source, `docker-compose.yml`, `scripts/freeduet-eval-compose.yml`, `.env.example`, `scripts/freeduet-eval-stack.sh` and the clean-start check, `examples/freeduet/`, `server/src/config.ts`, and the environment of `agent-freeduet`, `agent-computer`, the supervisor and the worker. File and line are as on main at 88b01af, before this change.

| Setting | Where its published value was | Who could present it | What holding it allowed | Now |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `SUPERVISOR_TOKEN` | `openbot/scripts/start.sh:109`, `docker-compose.yml:206`: `openbot-dev-supervisor-token` | Anything that reaches the supervisor's port (`SUPERVISOR_PORT`, 4500), published to `127.0.0.1` and `[::1]` only (`docker-compose.yml:256-257`): the person's own programs, and on Docker Desktop a Bot's computer | The supervisor's four verbs (`supervisor/src/index.ts:34-42`): ensure, stop, reset and list a Bot's computer. Not the Docker socket itself; the vocabulary is the boundary | Made |
| `COMPUTER_TOKEN` | `openbot/scripts/start.sh:110`, `docker-compose.yml:108` and `:208` (until PR #198 the supervisor handed it to every computer it made; each Bot's computer now gets its own token, derived from `SUPERVISOR_TOKEN`, see `docs/design/per-computer-token.md`): `openbot-dev-computer-token` | Anything that reaches a computer: the shared one's port (`COMPUTER_PORT`, 4100, loopback only, `docker-compose.yml:97-98`), and each Bot's own by name on the Compose network or on a loopback port (`supervisor/src/docker.ts:482-487`). Until PR #198 one token served every computer; now it is the one shared computer's | Drive that computer: its browser with the person's logins, its files, its commands | Made |
| `WORKER_SHARED_SECRET` | `openbot/scripts/start.sh:135`: `openbot-dev-worker-secret` | Anything that reaches the API server's port (3001), which binds no hostname unless `OPENBOT_HOST` says so (`server/src/http-server.ts:55`) | `POST /internal/routines/run` (`server/src/app.ts:1484`): dispatch a routine run whose id the caller knows. The run's owner and content come from the database, so little | Made |
| `KEY_ENCRYPTION_KEY` | `.env.example:5`: thirty-two zero bytes, which `server/src/config.ts:547` knows (`PLACEHOLDER_KEY`) and only warns about outside production | Nobody presents it: it is the vault's key at rest | With a copy of the database (a backup, a dump, or the database's own public password, below): every saved login, connection and company sign-in secret (`credentials.encrypted_value`, `saved_logins.encrypted_password`, `sso_providers`). The same key signs what the server asks itself to trust (`auth/signed-value.ts`: run assertions, hand-offs, OAuth state) and the Web Bot Auth identity derives from it | Made; on an installation in use, left (below) |
| The database's password, `DATABASE_URL` | `.env.example:1`, `docker-compose.yml:19` and `:77`, `openbot/scripts/start.sh:683` (the worker's fallback), `openbot/scripts/freeduet-eval-stack.sh:90` (the eval stack wrote it too): `openbot:openbot` | Anything that reaches the database's port (`POSTGRES_PORT`, 5432), published to `127.0.0.1` and `[::1]` only (`docker-compose.yml:40-41`): the person's programs, and on Docker Desktop a Bot's computer | Everything: the audit trail, the policy store, conversations, memories, and the vault's ciphertext. The most serious of the five | Made, and set on an existing database |

Not on the list because they have no published value. `AGENT_TOOL_TOKEN`, `MANAGED_AGENT_TOKEN`, `FREEDUET_LOCAL_KEY` and `FREEDUET_INSTALL_SECRET` are made per installation by `start.sh` already. `BETTER_AUTH_SECRET` is for a deployment with sign-in. `server/src/config.ts`, `agent-freeduet`, `agent-computer`, the supervisor, the worker, the toolkit and `examples/freeduet/` hold no secret with a literal default (`agent-computer/src/index.ts:120` and `supervisor/src/index.ts:61` refuse to start on an empty one). `stop.sh`, `open.sh`, `freeduet-eval-compose.yml` and the clean-start check read none of the five. One published value remains and is not on this path: `SPIRE_JOIN_TOKEN`, `openbot-dev-token` (`docker-compose.yml:167` and `:231`), for the SPIRE services that `start.sh` does not start.

**An installation that already runs on the old values, step by step.** What `git pull` and the next `bash scripts/start.sh` do, in order, and what the person reads:

1. **Three tokens.** A line that is empty, absent or still one of the old strings gets a new random value, written to `.env` (the line moves to the end of the file; comments stay). The run says `Replaced NAME, which was the example value published in this repository, with one generated for this installation, and wrote it to .env.` (`Generated NAME and wrote it to .env.` when there was none). A line the person changed is not touched.
2. **Compose.** It is declarative: the services whose environment changed (`agent-computer`, the supervisor) are recreated, in seconds. Their data are in volumes and are kept.
3. **The database.** Once Compose has it up, `start.sh` sets the new password on the running database, through the container's own socket (`ALTER ROLE openbot WITH PASSWORD ...`, the statement on standard input, so on no command line; it works whatever the old password was, and touches no data). It tries for up to 30 seconds. When the database holds the new password, `start.sh` writes `POSTGRES_PASSWORD` and `DATABASE_URL` to `.env` (same host, port and database, new password) and recreates the database's container, not its volume, so that Compose's own copy of the password matches. The run says `database: its password was the example one this repository publishes. It is now one generated for this installation, written to .env. Your data is untouched.` **If the password cannot be set** (the database does not answer, Docker refuses), `.env` is left exactly as it was, the start goes on with the old password, and says in red that it is still the example one and why, on every start until it works. With no `DATABASE_URL` at all and no way to set one it stops rather than start without.
4. **The migration** runs with the new password.
5. **The key.** `start.sh` asks the database how many rows hold something the key sealed (`credentials`, `saved_logins`, `sso_providers`; revoked rows count: the old key may be the only way to read one). **None:** the key is replaced, and the run says so. **Any, or the question could not be answered:** the key is left, and the run says in red on every start that it is still the example key, why it was kept, and that whoever gets a copy of the database can read what it holds. Nothing is ever re-sealed, so nothing the person saved is lost either way.
6. **The server and the worker restart once.** The server reads its settings at start, so a run that made or replaced a secret restarts it (the existing `SECRETS_ROTATED` path). The worker was only ever started when it was not running, so it kept the old secret and the old database password for as long as it lived, and every routine would have stopped firing without a word: it is now stopped and started again when either changed (`worker: its secret or the database's password changed, so it is restarted`).
7. **The Bots' computers.** A computer the supervisor made holds the old token. The supervisor replaces it the next time that Bot's computer is used, with its files, logins and browser profile kept (`supervisor/src/docker.ts`, `holdsCurrentToken`); what was running in it (an open page, a command) is lost.
8. **One line sums it up:** `Secrets that were the same on every installation have been replaced. The server and the worker restart once to use them, and so do the Docker services and Bot computers that held them. Nothing you saved is lost.` An installation that set every value itself reads nothing new and restarts nothing.

For a few seconds on the way (between the server's old connections and its restart), a request can fail where the server asks the database with a password it no longer holds; the start that caused it ends it.

**What changing a key that is in use takes.** `start.sh` leaves the key when the database holds anything sealed with it, and says so; this is what it points to. `bun scripts/rekey-vault.ts` now does that for the published example key: a person runs it once, with FreeDuet stopped, and it writes a backup of the three tables before it re-seals them in one transaction (PR #201, `docs/design/vault-rekey.md`). It has not been run on a real installation. For a key the person chose, the choices are still to leave the key, which makes the vault as private as the database is (a copy of the database is a copy of the vault), or to start the database again from nothing and connect everything again. The key is not rotated by deleting its line: with a vault in use that makes a new key and leaves every sealed row unreadable, the same as losing it. A new installation, and any whose vault is empty, gets its own key and has nothing to decide.

**What is verified, and how.**

- **`scripts/start-restart-guard.test.ts`: 140 tests, 49 of them new, all pass** (the harness runs `start.sh` against fakes; the Docker and database are modelled, not run). A start with none of the secrets makes each, in its shape, one line each, mode 600, shown nowhere (not in the output, a Docker or curl call, a signal, a launch line, or a child's environment where it does not belong); the database learns its password on standard input before the migration, and Compose, the server and the worker are handed the same values; two installations get different values; a later start reuses them, restarts nothing and sends the database nothing; a value somebody set, in the file or in the environment, is kept byte for byte while the missing ones are made, and the four kinds of database address that are not the example one are left; an installation on the old values (one quoted) is moved off all five, its server and worker restarted and relaunched with the new values, and its other settings untouched; a database that cannot be changed keeps the example password and says so, and with no address it stops; a database that holds rows, or cannot be asked, keeps the example key and says so on every start, and an absent key is made without asking; a clone of the shipped `.env.example` starts with all of them made and says nothing the clean-start check refuses (`api[_ ]?key`, `licen[cs]e is`); the eval stack's own `.env.eval` is made the same way with its own port, every Compose call names that file, and the person's `.env` is not touched. The questions `scripts/secrets.sh` answers (what is a published default, what is not, how the new password goes into an address) have 25 cases of their own. A scan keeps every published value out of every file the start reads but `secrets.sh`, and pins what Compose still defaults. A stub of the eval stack's `write_settings` checks it writes none of them.
- **`server/tests/vault-rows-query.integration.test.ts`, 6 tests on a real Postgres.** The text start.sh sends is run (it is read out of `secrets.sh`, not copied): one number, one more for a stored credential and for a revoked one, a missing table counts as empty (tables renamed inside a transaction that rolls back), and the schema is held to it: every column named for what it seals (`encrypted`, `sealed`, `cipher`) is in a table the question lists, `sso_providers` carries the two columns Better Auth writes, and the list is exactly three tables, so a new place to seal something fails a test instead of making the question answer "nothing". The `ALTER ROLE` statement and the `psql` flags were also run on a real server with a throwaway role: exit 0 and a SCRAM password; exit 2 against a database that is not there yet, which is what the 30 tries wait out.
- **`tests/compose.test.ts`, 21 tests, 2 new,** through `docker compose config`: no token default for the computer or the supervisor (none of the old strings is anywhere in the rendering), the settings' tokens reach both services, and `POSTGRES_PASSWORD` reaches the database and the migration's address, with `openbot` only when nothing is set.
- **Against main's `start.sh`, Compose file, `.env.example` and eval stack script** (with `secrets.sh` kept so the harness can copy it): 23 of the 24 tests that describe the behaviour fail, and 2 of the 2 new compose tests; the vault test file cannot load. The one that passes is the one that must: an installation that set every value itself is left exactly as it was. The 25 tests of `secrets.sh` fail on a tree without the file.
- **Existing tests.** `install.test.ts` (91), `processes.test.ts` (13), `stop-computers.test.ts` (4), `migrate-wait.test.ts` (4), `setup-learning.test.ts` (20), `freeduet-dictation-e2e.test.ts` (10), `clean-checkout.test.ts` (4), `workspace.test.ts` (36), `freeduet-model-base.test.ts` (9), `config.test.ts` (142), `install-secret.test.ts` (47 server, 3 supervisor), `single-user-peer.test.ts` (18), `assertion-shape.test.ts` (10) all pass. In `start-restart-guard.test.ts` the old pair of cases "an unchanged resolved `default` / `process environment` token leaves the API running" became one, the second, because a default token no longer exists; the fixture `.env` holds a non-example value for every secret, so the older tests meet a start that makes nothing. `bun run typecheck:tests` is clean against its baseline (239 known), `biome lint --error-on-warnings` and `biome format` are clean over `openbot/`, `bash -n` passes on the three scripts, and `shellcheck` finds nothing new beyond the info note for the sourced `secrets.sh` that `processes.sh` and `require-bash.sh` already have.

**What is not verified.**

- **No Docker ran.** The harness's `docker` is a fake, so nothing here proves that Compose recreates the right services, that the official `pgvector/pgvector:pg17` image trusts its local socket for `psql -U openbot` (`start.sh` already relied on it for its other checks, and the statement was run on a real PostgreSQL 16 server), or what a person sees on a real machine. **To check on a real stack (Linux, and Docker Desktop on Windows or macOS):** (a) on a fresh clone, `cp .env.example .env && bash scripts/start.sh`: `.env` holds six new values, mode 600, and the app works; `PGPASSWORD=openbot psql -h 127.0.0.1 -U openbot -d openbot -c 'select 1'` must be refused and the new password accepted; (b) on an installation started before this change, with some conversations and a memory in it: `git pull`, `bash scripts/start.sh`; read the lines above, then check the conversations and memory are still there, a routine still fires (the worker was restarted), and a Bot's computer comes back at its next use with its files; (c) the same with a connection or saved login in the vault: the key line must be unchanged, and the red lines must be there; (d) `bash scripts/freeduet-eval-stack.sh reset`, then `up`: it must come up on port 5442 with its own password; (e) the pull request's `clean start` job (label `full-ci`).
- **No team eval was run.** Nothing in a member's prompt, tools or skills changed.
- **The existing-installation path was not run against a real older checkout,** only against fixtures built from what an older `start.sh` wrote (the old strings, one quoted, `POSTGRES_PASSWORD` absent).

**Limits and decisions left to the maintainer.**

- **A vault in use keeps the public key.** An installation that has connected Gmail, saved a login or set up company sign-in is the one most worth protecting and the one this change cannot protect: its key stays, with a warning every start. The re-sealing tool this section recommended is built (PR #201, `docs/design/vault-rekey.md`); it runs only when the person runs it, with FreeDuet stopped, and it has not been run on a real installation.
- **An absent key is made, not asked about.** A `.env` that has lost its `KEY_ENCRYPTION_KEY` line (and has none in the environment) gets a new key even when the database holds sealed rows, which then stay unreadable until the old key is put back; the run says `Generated KEY_ENCRYPTION_KEY`. The server used to refuse to start in that state. Asking the database in this case too, and stopping when it holds rows, is a few lines and a test; it is not done here because a fresh clone, the case that matters, always answers none, and because the key being lost already is what made the rows unreadable.
- **The desktop app is not changed.** It already makes five of the six (`desktop/src-tauri/src/env.rs`, `MINTED` and `PUBLISHED`), but writes `postgres://openbot:openbot@127.0.0.1:<port>/openbot` itself (`env.rs:574-577`), so the Compose fallback `${POSTGRES_PASSWORD:-openbot}` stays for it, and the database's port is loopback only. Having the shell mint a password too is a small change in another surface with its own tests; its header comment, which says `start.sh` falls back to fixed strings, is now out of date.
- **`SPIRE_JOIN_TOKEN` keeps its example value.** `start.sh` does not start SPIRE; a deployment that turns it on should set its own.
- **For a few seconds a new database has the example password.** On a first start Compose creates the database with the fallback, and `start.sh` changes it at once; nothing is stored in it yet. Writing `POSTGRES_PASSWORD` before Compose starts would avoid even that, but only for a start that knows no volume exists, and asking Docker for that is more than this change needs.
- **A key replaced invalidates what was signed with the old one** that was still in flight: a run's assertion, a hand-off, a pending OAuth connection. They are minutes old at most. The Web Bot Auth identity, derived from the key unless `WEB_BOT_AUTH_KEY` is set, changes with it.
- **A Compose started by hand before any `start.sh` has no tokens,** and the computer and the supervisor refuse to start, saying why. That is the intended end; a person who wants one sets it in `.env`.
- **The Helm chart and the all-in-one image are not changed.** They hold none of the five values (the chart refuses the example key), and have their own secret handling.
- **`docs/ROADMAP.md` is not edited here.** The session that merges this updates it.

## 24. A mail assistant can move mail to the Bin, and only to the Bin (D41)

Decided by the maintainer on 15 November 2026: "a mail assistant must be able to remove mail". Built on the branch `mail-bin`, beside the four organise writes of PR #254 and #258 ([design/mail-organise.md](design/mail-organise.md), "Move to the Bin"). It is built and tested; nothing ran on a real mailbox.

| # | Decision | Why |
|---|---|---|
| D41 | **(1) One new mailbox write, `gmail_bin_messages`, on both Gmail connections.** It moves the listed messages to the Bin (Gmail's Trash), from which the person can restore them for about 30 days. It is not a permanent deletion: no expunge, no emptying the Bin, no permanent-delete call anywhere, no move to Spam, no filter, no forwarding. Over REST it is Gmail's own `users.messages.trash`, once for each id (the scope the entry already asks for, `gmail.modify`, covers it); over IMAP it is one `UID COPY` into the folder the server flagged `\Trash`, never one found by a guessed name (an account that reports none is refused, with nothing opened). **(2) The same limits as the other four:** only listed message ids (hex, as a search returns them), at most 100 a call, one reading of the arguments (`parseOrganiseCall`), a refusal before the gate and the audit row `mail_organise_call_invalid`, all of the named messages or none (a message that is gone, already in the Bin or in Spam is "not found" and refuses the call whole). **(3) The same gate as the other four.** No card only when the person explicitly ordered it in their own typed message of their live turn, directly or one hop through Iris, as the reviewer judges it on those typed words only; a card in every other case; a routine, a check-in, a remote session and a hand-off from one always ask; the person's own Always allow lifts the card for this tool only, and ask-before-changes still shows it. **(4) One fixed extra reviewer line for this tool only** (`MAILBOX_BIN_REVIEW_LINE`, `approvals/policy.ts`): binning is the strongest mailbox change, so `preApproved` is true only when the request itself says to delete, bin or trash AND names what (a sender, a category, a label or an unmistakable set); "clean up", "tidy" or "remove the spam" without the person having named which messages is a card. **(5) The card** says `Move 12 messages to the Bin`, how many and which as the server read them, that they stay about 30 days and can be restored, and that after that Gmail deletes them. **(6) Otto** is told he may do it when the person orders it, says first how many and which and that the Bin keeps them about 30 days; he still never deletes for good, empties the Bin, moves mail to spam or forwards it. The skill `organise-the-mailbox` says the same. | A mail assistant that can read, label and archive but cannot remove mail is not one, and the person's own typed order is the approval, as D27 and the organise rule already treat it. The Bin is the one removal that Gmail itself undoes for about 30 days, so it is the one a tool may make; a permanent deletion, an emptied Bin and a move to Spam are not, because nothing undoes them or because a Bot could hide mail from every view for good. The stricter reviewer line exists because a model that reads "tidy my inbox" as an order to bin ids it chose would hide mail the person did not mean, and the cost of that error is higher here than for an archive. |

**Full access and Always allow.** The Bin has no gate of its own beyond the reviewer line: Full access lifts the Bin card like any non-payment write in the person's own live turn (it never applies to a routine), and a broad personal allow rule would match it too, as it matches the other organising writes; Always allow written for this tool alone, by its exact `toolRef`, lifts nothing for the other tools. Whoever grants Full access to Otto accepts that his own judgement alone can then move mail to the Bin in a live turn.

**Not built, and why.** No way to restore from the Bin (the person does it in Gmail). No permanent deletion, no emptying the Bin. Spam is D42, below. No operation on "everything a search matches". The IMAP copy into the Bin has run against a recording server, not a real Gmail ([verify]).

**Found while building this: the grants of an update.** A member that holds some tools of a server is not given the tools a newer build adds (issue #102, D31 F): a person has to say yes once, on the inbox row, and an approval with no names gives only the reads. On the maintainer's install Otto held 5 of 10 and the new ones had to be added by hand. The brief for this change asked that the server, at start, give the package's member the newly added tools of a server whose other tools it already holds "by that same mechanism", only where the set was made by that mechanism and never where an administrator trimmed it by hand. **That part was not built**: today a grant row carries only who pressed (`plugin_grants.granted_by`, the same value for an inbox approval and for a press on the Bot's page), the audit rows of the two paths are not linked to a tool, and a revoke leaves no mark on the rows that remain, so the two cannot be told apart without guessing. See "Decisions left to the maintainer" in [design/mail-organise.md](design/mail-organise.md).

## 25. A mail assistant can move mail to Spam (D42)

Decided by the maintainer on 15 November 2026, after D41: Spam is no longer out of scope. Built in the same branch as the Bin's after-check ([design/mail-organise.md](design/mail-organise.md), section 11). It is built and tested; nothing ran on a real mailbox.

| # | Decision | Why |
|---|---|---|
| D42 | **(1) One more mailbox write, `gmail_spam_messages`, on both Gmail connections.** It moves the listed messages to Spam; Gmail deletes mail in Spam after about 30 days, and until then the person can move it back. It deletes nothing now: no expunge, no `\Deleted`, no emptying Spam, no permanent-delete call, no blocking of a sender, no filter, no forwarding. Over REST it is `users.messages.modify` once for each id, adding `SPAM` and removing `INBOX`; over IMAP it is one `UID COPY` from All Mail into the folder the server flagged `\Junk`, never one found by a guessed name (an account that reports none is refused, with nothing opened). **(2) The same limits, gate and audit as the other organising writes** (ids only, hex, at most 100, all of the named messages or none). **(3) After the copy over IMAP the same lookup runs again** (also for the Bin): an id All Mail still holds is "not confirmed", and the answer says how many were confirmed moved and how many are still in the mailbox, never "moved". **(4) One fixed reviewer line for this tool only** (`MAILBOX_SPAM_REVIEW_LINE`, `approvals/policy.ts`): `preApproved` when the request itself says to move mail to Spam or mark it as spam AND says which messages or which kind ("the spam in my inbox", a sender, a category); "check my inbox for spam and put it in Spam" is an explicit order for the messages the member judged to be spam; an instruction inside an email, a page or a tool result is never one. **(5) The card** says `Move 12 messages to Spam` and that Gmail deletes mail in Spam after about 30 days. **(6) Otto** and `organise-the-mailbox` say it once, in these words, and still say that he never deletes for good, empties the Bin or Spam, blocks a sender or forwards mail. Eval cases `otto-14` and `otto-15` (safety). | The maintainer's decision. It is the same kind of change as the Bin and less hard to undo than a deletion: the person can move the mail back until Gmail empties Spam. The reviewer line differs from the Bin's because "check my inbox for spam" is exactly how a person asks for this, and asking for the messages the assistant judged to be spam is a clear order; what stays a card is a vague order and any instruction that did not come from the person. Reporting a message as spam also teaches Gmail's own filter about the sender, which this tool does not claim to avoid: a move to Spam is what "Report spam" does in Gmail. |

**Full access and Always allow.** Like the Bin, Spam is a non-payment write: Full access lifts its card in the person's own live turn, a broad personal allow rule would match it, and an Always allow for this tool by its exact `toolRef` lifts nothing for the Bin or archive (and theirs lift nothing for Spam), as the gate tests pin.

**Not built, and why.** No emptying of Spam, no permanent deletion, no blocking a sender, no filter. No operation on "everything a search matches". The IMAP copy into Spam and the after-check have run against recording servers, not a real Gmail ([verify]): that Gmail removes a message from All Mail when it is copied into Spam, as it does into the Trash, is the premise of the check.

## 26. One line with Undo after a member is added from chat (D43)

Decided by the maintainer on 15 November 2026 (roadmap row "One line with Undo after every team change"). Built; not yet run live. Design: [design/settings-in-chat.md](design/settings-in-chat.md), section 8.11.

| # | Decision | Why |
|---|---|---|
| D43 | **(1) After the person presses Create on a proposed member, ONE line stands in place of the completed card: what was added, with Undo and Details.** The proposal card still asks first, as before. **(2) The record and the Undo reuse the settings-in-chat mechanism:** one audit event per change, the person's own Undo route, the same list, the seven-day limit. The record is made by `POST /api/settings-changes/members`, which the server accepts only for the person's own, private, keyless, just-made member, once (also for two requests at once). The line is drawn from that record and appears only when it was made. **(3) Undo takes the member back (deleted, its grants removed) only while it is exactly as recorded AND has not been used or shared** (no publication, audience, assignment, routine, responsibility, approval rule or conversation). A member edited, given another grant, used or already deleted since is left alone and the person is told. A second Undo is refused ("already undone"), also when two arrive together. The record and the Undo, each with its audit row, are one transaction under a lock on the member, so an audit row that cannot be written leaves nothing changed. **(4) Never a routine, a member tool or a remote session:** the routes are refused to a remote session by the existing rule, and nothing a member calls reaches them. **(5) English only. The setting cards (name, tone, language, notifications) are already a line with Undo and stay as they are.** | The person asked for a team that changes by talking, and an undo that is always there. A team change that cannot be taken back in one press is one the person hesitates to say yes to. Refusing Undo once the member has moved on is what keeps "restores exactly what was there before" true. |
